L1889: 3. CMC
L1890: Based on several outstanding deficiencies, including resolution of the issues concerning impurities present in the droxidopa drug substance, the CMC reviewer is not recommending approval of droxidopa. The CMC reviewer is currently waiting for responses to Information Requests from the holder of DMF (b) (4) and from the NDA applicant.
L1891: There are no recommended Phase 4 commitments, agreements or risk management steps.
L1892: Droxidopa, L-threo-3,4-dihydroxyphenylserine, is a small molecule with molecular formula C_9H_{11}NO_5 and molecular weight 213.19. The drug substance is manufactured by two suppliers, (b) (4) (DMF (b) (4) and (b) (4).
L1893: The change in droxidopa synthesis from the original process occurred in response to the Agency's request to reduce the (b) (4) in the drug substance to a value below (b) (4) based on the maximum anticipated drug dose of (b) (4).
L1894: Droxidopa manufactured by the (b) (4) process and by the (b) (4) process will be used to manufacture the U.S. commercial drug product; drug substance produced by the (b) (4) method was used in the clinical trials and in the primary stability studies.
L1895: Final reports of facility inspections are pending.
L1896: 4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L1897: The pharmacology-toxicology review team found droxidopa to be approvable.
L1898: Single oral doses of droxidopa increased BP in rats, rabbits and cats, but not in dogs. No repeat-dose studies evaluated BP effects to assess durability of effect. Interference with L-aromatic-amino acid decarboxylase (e.g., effect of carbidopa in PD patients treated with L-dopa) or interference with alpha-adrenergic receptor function diminishes or abolishes the pressor effect of droxidopa. Droxidopa did not affect heart rate, intestinal motility, CNS or respiratory functions. A hERG assay was negative.
L1899: Nonclinical pharmacokinetic data were limited to single-dose studies. Excretion of droxidopa in animals occurred mainly through the urine. Studies with radiolabeled droxidopa revealed six main peaks or metabolites in serum and urine: droxidopa parent, 3-OM-DOPS, vanillic acid (conjugated and non-conjugated) and protocatechuic acid (conjugated and non-conjugated). Pharmacokinetic evaluation of human data did not include serum metabolites, but was limited to urinary metabolites in which similar ratios between humans and animal species were seen. The sponsor has argued that, with catecholamine metabolic pathways conserved in evolution, including for droxidopa and its 3-OM-DOPS metabolite, humans likely would produce serum metabolites similar to those seen in animals.
L1900: Two rodent carcinogenicity studies were judged to be negative; however, there is uncertainty as to whether toxicology study animals (including mice and rats used for carcinogenicity studies and rabbits used for reproductive toxicity studies) were exposed to all major human serum metabolites or to sufficient levels of those metabolites.
L1901: Reproductive toxicology studies in rats (Segment I and II) and Segment II study in rabbits were unremarkable.
L1902: Due to an absence of an adequate metabolic profile, particularly in rabbits and mice, species used for reproductive toxicity and carcinogenicity testing, respectively, a Quantitative Structure Activity Relationship (QSAR) analysis of the major known human metabolites was conducted by the CDER Computational Toxicology Group. Results showed that 3-OM-DOPS was predicted to produce fetal dysmorphogenesis in the rabbit, a species in which no metabolic data were available. Although the mouse was shown to produce 3-OM-DOPS, two other metabolites (vanillic acid [VA] and protocatechuic acid [PA]) were predicted positive for carcinogenicity. On the other hand, a single species is generally considered adequate and VA has been adequately tested in the rat. Vanillin is a naturally occurring substance in foods, with an average daily intake of 2.5 mg/kg/day and has GRAS status. Finally, given the consistent metabolite pattern across species, the sponsor concluded that mice were also likely exposed to VA. A similar argument was made with regard to PA.
L1903: Outstanding issues:
L1904: 1. While concerns regarding potentially genotoxic impurities appear to have been adequately addressed, some remaining issues concerning impurities have not been resolved.2. Based on in vitro and rat brain data, there is some evidence that DOPEGAL, a metabolite of droxidopa and norepinephrine, is neurotoxic. The doses of droxidopa needed to produce CNS levels sufficient to result in neuronal loss are not known. However, one of the pharmacology-toxicology reviewers (T. Papoian) concluded that the potential neurotoxicity has not been adequately assessed and suggested further studies in rats to address this question.3. There is literature suggesting that DOPAL (3,4-dihydroxyphenylacetaldehyde), a degradant of droxidopa and a metabolite of dopamine, is neurotoxic. DOPAL has been detected in plasma after oral administration of droxidopa and has been shown to be markedly elevated in some Parkinson's disease patients treated with droxidopa (Holmes, 2010). The sponsor questioned the accuracy of the Holmes paper. According to the sponsor, there was no evidence of neurotoxicity based on preclinical and clinical assessments.4. Animal toxicity studies showed variability between species, with renal tubular and cardiac myocyte toxicity demonstrated in all mice and rat studies, including single-dose studies and at doses that, based on body surface area, were similar to or lower than the highest recommended clinical dose of droxidopa. However, droxidopa was essentially nontoxic at the highest doses tested in 52-week dog studies and 13-week rhesus monkey studies, and the highest doses tested in dogs and monkeys were 30-fold higher than the highest recommended clinical dose. The reasons for these differences are not clear. The lack of findings in dogs and monkeys at large multiples of the human dose provides some reassurance, although the question remains as to the most relevant species for humans.5. Given the uncertainty related to the cardiac and renal findings in rodents and their relevance for humans, Dr. Jensen recommended that the ongoing clinical
L1905: study in renal-impairment patients be completed prior to final approval. Dr. Jensen also recommended that the sponsor collect cardiac troponin measurements.
L1906: 5. Clinical Pharmacology/Biopharmaceutics
L1907: According to clinical pharmacology/biopharmaceutics review, droxidopa can be approved, provided that the toxicology information on DOPAL is adequate and the observed Phase 3 effect size is clinically meaningful. Based on the OSI report, the BE results for the 300 mg capsule formulation was not acceptable and the new 300 mg capsule formulation cannot be approved.
L1908: In addition to the Phase 3 studies, the sponsor submitted a PK/food effect/pivotal BE study (101), four Phase 2 clinical studies and single and multiple-dose PK studies in healthy human volunteers. There were no dedicated renal and hepatic impairment studies, mass balance or drug-drug interaction studies in the development program. A population PK/PD and thorough QT study (102) were included in the submission.
L1909: • General clinical pharmacology/biopharmaceutics considerations, including absorption, metabolism, half-life, food effects, bioavailability, etc.
L1910: Droxidopa is an immediate-release oral capsule and synthetic catecholamine acid analogue that is converted to norepinephrine. Droxidopa is rapidly absorbed in healthy subjects with peak plasma concentrations by 2 hours post-dose; influence of pH or involvement of transporters on absorption was not studied. The pharmacokinetic profile is nearly dose-proportional from 100 to 600 mg. The terminal elimination half-life of droxidopa ranged from 2.1-2.4 hours; the major metabolite 3-OM-DOPS also showed dose-dependent increase in exposure up to 600 mg, with an elimination half-life of 4.7-5.3 hours. No dose-dependent relationship could be attributed to NE. There was no evidence of accumulation with multiple doses of a three times daily (TID) regimen. The plasma protein binding for droxidopa is concentration-dependent, with decreases from 75 to 25% with increase in concentration from 0.1 to 10 µg/ml. The estimated apparent volume of distribution of droxidopa is about 200L in humans. Droxidopa crosses the blood-brain barrier in animals and humans.
L1911: The major active metabolite of droxidopa is norepinephrine. Other metabolites identified in humans and animals include methylated droxidopa (3-OM-DOPS), vanillic acid (VA) and protocatechuic acid (PA).
L1912: Moderate food effect was observed (AUC and Cmax decrease by 20% and 35%, respectively, and Cmax delayed about 2 hours with a high fat meal). The phase III trials were conducted without food restrictions; therefore, the moderate food effects observed with the FMI formulation was considered not to be clinically significant.
L1913: The exact mechanism of action of droxidopa is unknown. Droxidopa is a synthetic amino acid analog that is metabolized through the catecholaminergic metabolism system and releases norepinephrine (NE) in addition to several other metabolites. In humans,
L1914: droxidopa administration results in a transient increase in serum NE (&lt; 1 ng/mL). NE increases blood pressure via peripheral arterial and venous vasoconstriction and affects the central nervous system. Droxidopa crosses the blood-brain barrier; it is proposed that droxidopa affects NOH by acting both peripherally and centrally through NE release.
L1915: The proposed dose range is 100-600 mg three times daily, to be taken at 4 hour intervals during the day.
L1916: Exploratory analyses by the clinical pharmacology reviewer of patients stratified by the final dose from the open-label titration phase in 301 and 302 suggest a dose-response for OHSA item 1 or SBP and droxidopa 100-300 mg TID (see Clinical Pharmacology review, Figures 4 and 5 ). However, these analyses are limited by the lack of a concurrent placebo arm. In addition, since patients were not randomized to the different dose groups, one cannot exclude the possibility of imbalances between groups that could confound these results.
L1917: In vivo PK interactions of droxidopa and other drugs were not evaluated in this application. The Phase III program allowed concomitant carbidopa/levodopa, dopamine agonists, MOA-B and COMT inhibitors for patients with Parkinson's disease. DOPA decarboxylase inhibitors (DDC-I) increased the exposure of droxidopa by about 2-fold, and there were no significant changes in adverse events. However, patients not taking DDC-I with droxidopa showed numerically higher improvements in NOH symptoms and BP compared to patients on concomitant DDC-I. These data are not consistent in study 302 (difficult to understand whether the difference is due to the concomitant DDC-I in 301, withdrawal design in 302 or that patients on DDC-I might be a different, sicker population). The clinical pharmacology reviewer has suggested that there may be loss of efficacy from droxidopa if co-administered with DDC-I, since peripherally, DDC-I prevents the formation of NE from droxidopa, thereby reducing NE-mediated pharmacological activity. Droxidopa, unlike DDC-I, crosses the blood-brain barrier and perhaps further droxidopa studies might inform us as to the extent that droxidopa acts centrally vs. peripherally. Given the current uncertainty, this reviewer agrees that labeling a potential interaction between droxidopa and DDC-I seems appropriate.
L1918: Droxidopa is metabolized by non-CYP-mediated pathways and involves catecholamine systems in its metabolism. In vitro studies indicate that droxidopa has low CYP induction or inhibition potential. Animal data indicate that up to 70% of droxidopa and its metabolites are excreted in the urine. The sponsor measured plasma concentrations of droxidopa and its metabolites 3-OM-DOPS and NE in plasma. In a phase I clinical trial, the sponsor also measured vanillic acid, protocatechuic acid and their conjugates in human urine. Urinary excretion of all these analytes in humans accounts for only 44% (corrected for the molecular weight) of a 300 mg single oral dose of droxidopa. Since droxidopa is a substrate for the COMT pathway, the clinical pharmacology reviewer raised the possibility of additional droxidopa metabolites that were not quantified or identified by the sponsor.
L1919: No dose adjustment is required based on body weight, sex or age.
L1920: The current application does not include a dedicated renal impairment study; however the sponsor has planned a renal impairment PK study including mild, moderate, severe renal impairment and end-stage renal disease. The phase III program included NOH patients with mild and moderate renal impairment and dose titration seemed to have been unaffected by renal function; approximately 30% of patients, irrespective of renal function, received the highest dose of 600 mg. In addition, adverse event profiles in patients with mild/moderate renal impairment were similar to those with normal renal function. Therefore, no dose adjustment was recommended for patients with mild and moderate renal impairment.
L1921: The application did not include a dedicated hepatic impairment study; however, the metabolism of droxidopa is not CYP-mediated and it is unlikely that mild to moderate hepatic impairment will affect exposure; in addition, droxidopa did not affect markers of hepatic function (e.g., transaminases or bilirubin). Therefore, no dose adjustment is recommended in patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment.
L1922: According to population PK data, increasing age above 65 years was associated with a decrease in apparent clearance by 0.8%/year. There was no apparent effect of age on dose selection during the Phase III titration phases; however, patients over 75 years showed little to no benefit following droxidopa treatment on NOH symptoms or BP measurements. Since droxidopa is titrated to effect, those experiencing no symptom relief upon reaching the highest dose level are expected to discontinue treatment. There are no available pharmacokinetic data in children.
L1923: In a blinded, randomized four-period crossover study, 52 healthy subjects received single oral doses of droxidopa 600 mg, droxidopa 2000 mg, placebo and moxifloxacin 400 mg and there was no evidence of QT prolongation.
L1924: 6. Clinical Microbiology
L1925: This section is not relevant to this application.
L1926: 7. Clinical/Statistical- Efficacy
L1927: The sponsor submitted three clinical studies (301, 302 and 303) to support efficacy. Study 305 was an ABPM substudy of 301 and 304 (ongoing long-term extension study). Study 306 (ongoing) was not formally submitted to the NDA; however, the protocol was reviewed under IND 77248.
L1928: Study 301 was an induction-design, with an initial screening period (up to 14 days), open-label dose-titration (up to 14 days), followed by 7-day washout and 7-day double-blind randomized placebo-controlled treatment period.
L1929: Droxidopa was initially dosed at 100 mg TID, with up-titrating in 100 mg TID increments until 1. the patient became asymptomatic (score of “0” on OHSA item 1) AND had increase in
L1930: standing SBP 10 mm Hg relative to baseline; 2. sustained SBP &gt; 180 mm Hg or DBP &gt; 110 mm Hg; 3. intolerable side effects believed to be drug-related; 4. maximum dose of 600 mg TID reached. Responders were defined as those with improvement of at least 1 unit on OHSA item 1 (dizziness) and increase in SBP of at least 10 mm Hg at 3 minutes post-standing.
L1931: Responders (satisfying both BP and symptom criteria) were entered into the washout period and randomized at the droxidopa dose (or placebo) where they qualified as responder. Patients were instructed to complete the OHQ (composite of OHSA and OHDAS scores) at baseline, Visit 4 (randomization) and Visit 5 (Final study evaluation 7 days later). Global assessment evaluations were completed by the patient and clinician at baseline (CGI-S only) and at Visits 4 and 5.
L1932: The OHQ instructed patients to report the average severity of symptoms over the previous week.
L1933: The diagram illustrates the study design flow. It begins with V1 (SCREENING) leading to a 'Screening Period (Up to 14 Days)'. V2 (BASELINE) marks the start of the 'Open-Label Dose Titration (Up to 14 Days)'. During this titration (V3a, V3b, V3c, V3d, V3e, V3f), patients are titrated through doses of 100mg TID, 200mg TID, 300mg TID, 400mg TID, 500mg TID, and 600mg TID. Those who respond at the 'END OF TITRATION' enter the 'Wash Out Period (7 Days)'. V4 (RANDOMIZATION) occurs after the washout, with patients randomized to either 'Droxidopa' or 'Placebo'. V5 (FINAL EVALUATION) follows, leading to a 'Follow Up Period (7 Days)' and a final 'Follow Up' outcome.Flowchart of Study 301 design showing five visits (V1-V5) and corresponding study periods: Screening (V1), Baseline (V2), Open-Label Dose Titration (V3a-V3f), Wash Out (between V3f and V4), Double-Blind Randomized (V4), and Follow Up (V5).
L1934: TID=Three times daily.
L1935: Figure 1. Study 301 design Source: primary medical review (Dr. Blank)
L1936: Diabetics and patients with sustained severe hypertension were excluded; Parkinson’s disease medications were allowed.
L1937: The study did not control for non-pharmacologic interventions associated with management of orthostatic hypotension, such as salt/water intake, speed of changing from supine to standing, or compression stockings. Consequently, potential confounding due to these factors cannot be excluded.
L1938: In addition, patients were not allowed to lie flat while in the study; therefore, effects of droxidopa on supine hypertension were not assessed.
L1939: The primary endpoint was the change in mean composite OHQ; this endpoint was changed from OHSA item 1 (dizziness, lightheadedness, or feeling like you might black out) following analysis of Study 302, which failed on its primary endpoint. Based on study 302, the sponsor resized study 301 in order to achieve adequate power; according to the sponsor, study participants remained blinded to study results at the time of these changes.
L1940: Dr. Papadopoulos (SEALD) reviewed the OHQ and its component subscales (OHSA and OHDAS). The SEALD endpoint review concluded that the content validity of the OHQ is not well supported by the data provided in the submission, but that OHSA item 1 represented core symptoms of NOH and therefore could be used in labeling if droxidopa were approved. Since the recall period of all OHQ items was for the week prior to assessment, “response is likely to be influenced by the patient’s state at the time of recall” (SEALD review).
L1941: CGI-S and CGI-I are general tests of clinical illness that were used as additional global assessments. While it may be unclear how these scales could distinguish between NOH and other disease-related symptoms, the purpose of adding these scales was to address the question of clinically meaningful benefit. Dr. Papadopoulos, in the SEALD review, recommended that anchor-based methods using changes on the patient-reported CGI-S given the most weight for interpreting meaningful intra-patient changes on OHDAS item 1 (dizziness, lightheadedness, feeling faint or feeling like you might black out).
L1942: The randomized population was mostly Caucasian; about 50% female; with about 40% from the United States. The mean age was about 56 (18-87) years; the most common underlying etiology was Parkinson’s Disease (about 40%) followed by Pure Autonomic Failure (about 33%) and Multiple System Atrophy (about 15%). No patients with DBH deficiency were enrolled in this trial. The mean baseline OHQ score was 5.6 to 5.9 (range between 1.2 and 9.8); the mean baseline SBP standing 3 minutes was about 91 mm Hg (range 50-130 mm Hg). There were no gross imbalances between the two randomized groups.
L1943: About 40% of patients were on concomitant DOPA and DOPA derivatives during double-blind treatment. About 24% of patients were on concomitant mineralocorticoid treatment, without gross imbalances between the double-blind treatment groups.
L1944: Blinded safety data were presented to an independent DMC; DMC decisions regarding study conduct were based only on safety data.
L1945: Study 301 met its amended primary endpoint, with a statistically significant difference in OHQ score (below). Results for OHSA item 1 (dizziness) were consistent.

L1953: Source: primary medical review
L1954: That patients on placebo continue to improve OHQ score is likely due to a placebo effect, an effect on being in a clinical study, or a result of variability of the disease. One cannot exclude a delayed effect of droxidopa but, if this were true, one might consider a different dosing regimen that could (theoretically) provide a symptom benefit without the risk of hypertension.

L1958: Results of individual components of the OHSAS were internally consistent, with improvements in patients randomized to each treatment group and a larger effect in most items (except for concentration or head/neck discomfort) in droxidopa-treated patients.
L1959: Cumulative distribution curves of OHSAS item 1 are also consistent.

L1962: Source: ISE Figure 4-4
L1963: The CGI-S (clinical global impressions-severity) and CGI-I (clinical global impressions-improvement) scales, while showing a positive trend, showed no statistically significant differences between droxidopa and placebo. A large, statistically significant effect on CGI scores would have supported a clinically meaningful effect. On the other hand, it is not clear what “power” would be needed to detect a statistically significant difference in CGI scores. A significant difference in CGI scores was not a prespecified requirement to meet the primary endpoint.
L1964: Subgroup analyses of the OHQ composite score showed larger effects in patients &lt; 65 years; in males; in non-US populations; and in patients with PAF (there were too few patients with NDAN to draw conclusions).3 One can also observe a smaller effect size in PD patients.

L1973: Source ISE: Figure 4-7
L1974: Study 302 was designed with an open-label dose titration (100-600 mg TID) that was similar to Study 301. After titration, patients underwent a 7-day open-label treatment period, followed by randomization to 14-day double-blind treatment with either the same dose of droxidopa or placebo. The primary efficacy endpoint was the mean change from randomization to end of study visit in OHSA item 1; patients were instructed to rate these symptoms on average over the past week. Secondary endpoints had no prespecified hierarchy and included BP measurements 3 minutes post-standing; global assessment scales (CGI-S and CGI-I); and OHSA and OHDAS scores.
L1975: Of those randomized, baseline characteristics were similar between droxidopa and placebo groups. The mean ages were early-mid 60 years; the population was mostly Caucasian (&gt;90%); majority (about 60%) male; and about 50-60% U.S.-based. As with study 301, about 45% had underlying Parkinson’s disease and about 30% MSA; one patient with DBH deficiency was randomized to placebo. Mean baseline OHQ composite scores were about 6.0, but ranged from 0.9 to 9.6; mean baseline OHSA 1 score was 6.3-6.6, but ranged from 2-10.
L1976: The study failed to meet its primary endpoint; both groups, including droxidopa, showed worsening in OHSA item 1. It is not clear whether this worsening is a function of the OHSA testing; variability of the disease and concomitant conditions; or other factors. One would have expected patients remaining on droxidopa treatment to maintain any symptom improvement; in this regard, worsening of OHSA item 1 in droxidopa-treated patients is an unexpected finding.
L1977: SBP results are shown below. From randomization to the end of study, mean SBP during the orthostatic standing test decreased by 7.6 mm Hg (from 106.3 to 98.8 mm Hg) in patients who remained on droxidopa; there was no significant difference from placebo.

The x-axis is labeled "Timepoint" and includes four points: Baseline, EOT, Rand, and EOS. L1979: Source: medical review, Table 16, displayed graphically
L1980: In study 302, exploratory analysis showed a statistically significant difference in OHQ score between droxidopa with an effect size of 0.96 (p=0.042) with both groups worsening, placebo more than droxidopa. The patient-rated CGI-S score also showed a statistically significant difference favoring droxidopa. Subgroup analysis of OHQ composite scores showed a statistically significant improvement in patients with PAF. Based on the statistically significant OHQ scores, the primary endpoint in Study 301 was changed, under blinded conditions, to the change in OHQ score.
L1981: Study 303 was designed as a 3-month open-label treatment followed by 2 week double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized withdrawal, followed by 9 month open-label treatment. All study patients were previously enrolled in studies 301 or 302. The primary efficacy endpoint was the change in OHQ scores. Of note, study 303 (N randomized = 75) had only 50% power to detect a difference between treatment arms. It is not clear why the study was underpowered.
L1982: According to the study report, “a positive trend was considered to be evidence of durability of effect.”
L1983: The study did not meet its primary endpoint. Results for OHQ composite scores are shown below; it is not clear why the mean OHQ composite score for droxidopa-treated patients worsened during the double-blind period.
L1984: It is also not clear why the mean standing SBP decreased in patients who remained on droxidopa during the double-blind withdrawal period; the results seem paradoxical (unless there is an error). At the end of the double-blind period, the mean of neither group approached baseline.

L1988: Source: Table 24, primary medical review, results displayed graphically
L1989: Study 306 is an ongoing parallel group, 8-week double-blind study comparing droxidopa and placebo in Parkinson’s disease patients with symptomatic NOH. The primary endpoint of 306A was the OHQ composite score. According to Dr. Freidlin’s review under IND 77,248, the DMC recommended stopping 306 for futility based on the interim analysis. However, after reviewing results from the other secondary endpoints, the DMC recommended continuing study 306 with a new primary endpoint and increase in sample size; the primary endpoint for study 306B is the rate of patient-reported falls. Dr. Freidlin concluded that study 306 may be useful to support safety and generate hypotheses, but could not be considered as supporting efficacy in the NDA submission. (CDTL reviewer comment: Falls are a poor surrogate for symptomatic orthostatic hypotension, since Parkinson’s disease patients might fall for other reasons, such as dyskinesia or gait freezing).
L1990: Study design timeline for Study 306. It shows phases: Visit 1 Screening (2 weeks), Visit 2 Randomization, Titration (Up to 2 weeks) with visits 3a, 3b, 3c, etc., Treatment (8 weeks) with visits 4, 5, 6, and Visit 8 Follow Up (2 weeks). Two groups are shown: Droxidopa, TID (titrated from 100 mg to 600 mg) and Placebo, TID (titrated from 100 mg to 600 mg).
L1991: Figure 2. Study 306 design Source: medical officer review (Dr. Gordon), IND 77248, 4/14/2010.
L1992: Nonetheless, results from study 306 could prove informative regarding efficacy/hypotheses, and at the very least generating 8-week placebo-controlled data with background event rates.
L1993: • Notable efficacy issues both resolved and outstanding
L1994: According to Dr. Zhang (statistics), only one study appears to demonstrate efficacy of droxidopa (301) and an additional study is needed to confirm the finding in Study 301.
L1995: Dr. Blank, the primary medical reviewer, recommended that approval not be granted on the basis of safety concerns; and lack of demonstrable durability of symptom benefit.
L1996: I concur with the medical and statistical reviewers. Only one study, 301, met its primary endpoint with a statistically significant result (p=0.003) for a symptomatic claim, with a level of significance that does not meet the low p-value criterion to replace the need for “two study” substantiation. Acknowledgement that the disease can be disabling, with few pharmacologic alternatives, should not lead to a lowering in regulatory standards.
L1997: Concerns were raised about content validity for OHQ composite score; even if one accepted the OHQ score, or OHSA item 1, there is uncertainty regarding clinical meaningfulness of the effect size; the global scale did not show a consistently statistically significant, although one wonders whether the study was adequately powered for this particular measurement. It is possible that the retrospective assessment of symptoms or sporadic nature of the condition affected results; and one cannot exclude the potentially confounding effects of concomitant non-pharmacologic therapy or fluid status.
L1998: An additional concern is durability, since treatment of NOH is a chronic condition and evidence of durability has not been shown. It is not clear why study 303 was underpowered; however, the BP and symptom results are not consistent with results of 301. Study 306 was not submitted as part of this application; since approval decisions should be based on the totality of the information, it would have been helpful to further review data from this trial.
L1999: This reviewer would therefore like to see an additional efficacy trial, ideally in PAF patients, with real-time (e.g., not retrospective) assessment, placebo-controlled data lasting longer than one week and evidence of long-term efficacy.
L2000: 8. Safety
L2001: Safety data are based on two Phase 3 controlled studies, 301 and 301; two open-label extension studies, 303 and 304; an ABPM study (305) in a subset of study 301; a PK/food effect study in healthy volunteers (101); one single-dose QT study in healthy volunteers (102) and an ongoing double-blind placebo-controlled study in Parkinson’s disease patients (study 306: total planned sample size 211).
L2002: A total of 778 subjects (658 patients in Phase 2/3 studies and 120 healthy volunteers in Phase 1 studies) were exposed to droxidopa doses ranging from 100 to 1800 mg/day in Chelsea and Dainippon Sumitomo Pharma-sponsored studies. In the Chelsea development program, a total of 752 patients were exposed to droxidopa in doses ranging from 300-1800 mg/day;
L2003: however, only 44 patients were exposed to 1800 mg/day of droxidopa, the highest dose, for at least 6 months, and only 26 patients were exposed to this dose for at least one year. The data inform us that 26 people were able to tolerate high-dose droxidopa for at least one year; however, if one is concerned about long-term, dose-related adverse events, this database will only exclude large safety signals (&gt; about 11%, using the “rule of three”). However, if droxidopa use is indeed limited to a small “rare disease” population, and there is no reason to worry about wider use, then the size of the database seems reasonable.
L2004: It is difficult to interpret the adverse event profile for the following reasons:
L2005: 1. We generally consider a control group, such as a group treated with placebo, to evaluate background event rates in the study population. However, all patients in studies 301 and 302 were exposed to droxidopa prior to receiving placebo. Although droxidopa has a short half-life, the duration of droxidopa’s pharmacodynamic effects is not clear; thus, how long do patients need to be off droxidopa before meriting a consideration of “true placebo controls?”2. Even if one believes that the placebo group represents a “true placebo,” the double-blind study design lends comparability to a control group for, at best, 1-2 weeks. Since droxidopa is proposed as treatment for a chronic disease with complications, one cannot adequately evaluate risks, compared to background events, beyond 2 weeks of therapy.▪ Study 306 could have been an opportunity to obtain 8 weeks of placebo-controlled data in Parkinson’s disease patients.3. The effect on droxidopa on the risk of supine hypertension, a known complication of NOH, has not been well characterized. A corollary concern is that the cardiovascular risks of droxidopa, including risks related to supine hypertension (or, for that matter, chronic norepinephrine use) have not been well characterized.▪ The primary medical reviewer has noted 3 strokes (2 postmortem); 3 hypertensive crisis adverse events (AE); 1 fatal MI; 1 report of CAD that led to discontinuation. A relationship to droxidopa is plausible; however, it is difficult to interpret the incidence relative to background rate.4. This limited safety database does not adequately address a theoretical concern regarding potential neurotoxicity of DOPEGAL, a metabolite of droxidopa. It is not clear whether the clinical program evaluated subtle neurologic changes over the short double-blind period.
L2006: Droxidopa has been approved in Japan since 1989 but is marketed at lower doses, 100-300 mg TID, than the proposed 100-600 mg TID doses in the US program. Consequently, a lack of significant safety signals in the postmarketing Japanese database does not provide reassurance concerning dose-related safety concerns.
L2007: Postmarketing data in Japan were collected through retrospective postmarketing surveys and spontaneous AE reporting by health care providers. Dr. Blank reviewed 9 cases of neuroleptic
L2008: malignant syndrome (NMS) in the Japanese postmarketing experience. Of these cases, 4 were Parkinson's disease, 2 were Shy-Drager, 1 MSA, 1 striatonigral degeneration and 1 spinocerebellar degeneration. During the advisory committee meeting, the sponsor reviewed their cases of NMS and found 2 cases of NMS that remained unexplained.
L2009: It is difficult to interpret the cases of NMS, a rare but potentially life-threatening event. Since one is relying on the quality of retrospective documentation, one cannot exclude the possibility of misclassification bias; it is also possible that the NMS cases did not include "culprit" concomitant medication or precipitant. However, the existing safety database is not large enough to reassure against the possibility of a rare event such as NMS related to droxidopa.
L2010: There were 18 deaths in the Chelsea development program; a majority of these deaths (11) occurred in patients with MSA. One death occurred in a patient who did not receive droxidopa. Five of these cases involved a diagnosis of aspiration pneumonia. Two cases were complications of hip/pelvis fractures, presumably from falls. Two deaths were from stroke (via autopsy results). It is difficult to interpret these findings without a background rate in a concurrent control population.
L2011: The serious adverse event rates were low in the 1-2 week placebo-controlled phase, with 2 SAE reported in one placebo patient (mental status change and urinary tract infection). During the open-label titration phase, three patients discontinued study drug for SAE (nausea/vomiting; coronary artery disease; and pneumonia, respectively).
L2012: In study 301, a total of 13 patients in the open-label phase discontinued due to AEs. The most common AEs leading to discontinuation were nausea (3 patients); hypertension (3 patients); and vomiting, asthenia, irritability, dizziness, tremor, palpitations, increased BP and diabetes mellitus. Another patient at screening was identified as having hypertensive episodes and discontinued with palpitations and headache AEs.
L2013: In study 302, 13 patients in the open-label phase and 2 placebo-treated patients in the double-blind phase had AEs leading to study discontinuation. In the open-label phase, dizziness was the most common AE leading to discontinuation. Two placebo-treated patients in the double-blind phase discontinued due to loss of consciousness/syncope.
L2014: Compared to placebo, droxidopa therapy was associated with a higher incidence of increased BUN and creatinine; however, there were no concerning renal adverse event signals.
L2015: The primary medical reviewer recommended against approval based on the small safety database and worrisome safety signals in the open-label phase, including deaths, strokes, MI, progression of underlying disease and hypertensive crisis; as well as the Japanese cases of NMS, some unexplained by concomitant medications.
L2016: From this reviewer's perspective, the safety data in this submission are difficult to interpret; given the lack of a control. For example, it is not clear whether progression of underlying disease is an expected event or exacerbated by droxidopa. Strokes, MI and hypertensive crisis
L2017: are plausible events with catecholamine use, but might also occur spontaneously, especially in a population prone to supine hypertension.
L2018: Additional controlled data would be helpful in interpreting risks. In this regard, the ongoing controlled study in Parkinson's disease patients could prove informative. Furthermore, if droxidopa has undergone development for other indications, or if there is some potential droxidopa benefit in a different patient population, placebo-controlled droxidopa trials could also be informative, although the other populations might not be as sick (or vulnerable) as the NOH population.
L2019: This reviewer would like additional longer-term placebo-controlled (or even active-controlled, perhaps with a midodrine control group) studies in order to evaluate, against some background, the adverse events encountered in this development program.
L2020: If droxidopa were to be approved, and the only concern is BP, one potential strategy is to label for BP monitoring (with discontinuation of droxidopa if the blood pressure is uncontrolled).
L2021: 9. Advisory Committee Meeting
L2022: At an advisory committee meeting on February 23, 2012, salient discussions involved the following issues:
L2023: 1. Consistency of the blood pressure effect and questionnaire results;2. Validation of the PRO measurement;3. Whether the effect size was clinically meaningful and whether the p-value was low enough to satisfy evidence of effectiveness in one study; the panel statistician felt that in one positive study, "a large effect was more important than a low p-value."4. Some panel members were concerned about durability of effect and adequate demonstration of safety, especially with regard to supine hypertension;5. Other panel members felt that efficacy was adequately demonstrated in a subgroup and hypertension was a monitorable side effect;6. In the rare subgroup of dopamine beta-hydroxylase deficiency, with younger lower-risk patients, the safety database might be compatible with approval. Since no specific DBH deficiency data were presented, this conclusion appeared to be based on a patient testimonial presented during the open public hearing, as well as the comment that DBH deficiency represented a "pure" lack of norepinephrine.7. In response to the sponsor's proposal for a registry, members expressed concern about ability for signal detection; felt a need for a control group, but thought that a registry could address down-titration, supine hypertension, could include nonresponders and background therapies.
L2024: Several patients spoke in favor of droxidopa during the open public session; all speakers acknowledged transportation and hotel fees paid by the sponsor.
L2025: The committee voted 7-4 in favor of approval, with one member abstaining and another member leaving prior to the vote. A majority of the remaining panelists were convinced that droxidopa was efficacious in an identifiable subset of patients; in addition, the expected risks (hypertension) were measurable and predictable. Four panelists felt that the current data were insufficient; an additional confirmatory trial with safety and durability data was needed to support a claim.
L2026: 10. Pediatrics
L2027: Droxidopa was granted orphan designation and is therefore exempt from the requirement for pediatric studies.
L2028: 11. Other Relevant Regulatory Issues
L2029: Five clinical sites (103, 105 in the US; 505, 507 and 513 in the Ukraine) and the sponsor were inspected by the Good Clinical Practice Assessment Branch, Division of Good Clinical Practice Compliance, Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI). The inspectional findings at the 3 sites in the Ukraine were minor and OSI does not consider these violations likely to influence data integrity, study outcome or subject safety. The inspectional findings at site 105 (Texas) were also minor and isolated and not affecting study or data outcome. There were no regulatory violations identified at site 103 (Arizona) or at the Sponsor site. OSI recommended that the data submitted by the sponsor be used in support of the indication.
L2030: The Controlled Substance Staff reviewed adverse events in the clinical program for signals of abuse, dependence and withdrawal-related symptoms and concluded that there is no significant abuse potential associated with the use of droxidopa.
L2031: 12. Labeling
L2032: • The brand name NORTHERA was originally accepted by the FDA Division of Medical Error Prevention and Analysis (DMEPA) on December 29, 2008.• Should droxidopa be approved, an unresolved issue is whether droxidopa, like midodrine, merits a “boxed warning” regarding supine hypertension.• Another unresolved labeling issue is how to label instructions for use, given the lack of demonstrated long-term efficacy and potential risks.
L2033: 13. Recommendations/Risk Benefit Assessment
L2034: This reviewer recommends a complete response, with the need for an additional efficacy trial and additional controlled safety data.
L2035: • Risk Benefit Assessment
L2036: Droxidopa was studied in a heterogeneous population of patients with symptomatic NOH. From a selected population of responders who tolerated the drug, droxidopa increased SBP and met its primary endpoint in study 301, with consistency in most of the OHQ components; however, these results were not substantiated in the other two studies.
L2037: There is uncertainty as to validity of the primary endpoint instrument or how clinically meaningful the result. The global scales (included to support the OHQ interpretation) but did not show statistically significant improvement.
L2038: According to the SEALD reviewer, concordance between the OHSA Item 1 and the patient-reported global assessment was poor. The SEALD could not state with confidence a minimal clinically meaningful intrapatient change in OHSA item 1.
L2039: The medical and statistical reviewers did not recommend approval. The medical reviewer felt that study 301 showed a treatment effect; however, the application showed no evidence of durability in a chronic condition, and the safety review showed signals of concern. The statistical reviewer concluded that an additional trial was needed to confirm the results of study 301. I concur with both reviewers; however, I find the safety results to be difficult to interpret without a concurrent placebo group of some duration longer than one week.
L2040: Without demonstration of durability of benefit, one can potentially label droxidopa for “one week of benefit” with uncertainty beyond a week. But how should a health care provider prescribe droxidopa? Does the patient need to take the medication three times daily, or is compliance related to occurrence of symptoms and need for medication (like “PRN” pain medications)?
L2041: • Recommendation for Postmarketing Risk Evaluation and Management Strategies
L2042: The sponsor submitted a proposed Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS), including a Medication Guide, communication plan, and timetable for submission of assessments. The Division of Risk Management concluded that the risk of supine hypertension can be managed through labeling and a REMS is not warranted. However, if droxidopa were to be approved, the sponsor may voluntarily implement a DHCP letter and additional voluntary measures (b) (4)
L2043: . The Medication Guide should be included in labeling, with language informing patients to elevate the head of their bed to minimize risk of supine hypertension. If the supine hypertension risk associated with droxidopa is comparable to that of midodrine, a boxed warning should be considered. The Division of Epidemiology should be consulted to obtain input and evaluate feasibility regarding the proposed Phase IV registry.
L2044: This reviewer concurs.
L2045: • Recommended Comments to Applicant
L2046: 1. Need for additional efficacy data, perhaps in a subset with the largest effect (such as PAF population);2. Need for additional safety data, including but not limited to study 306.

L2065: 1 Recommendations/Risk Benefit Assessment
L2066: 1.1 Executive Summary and Recommendation on Regulatory Action
L2067: The applicant is seeking approval of Droxidopa for “the treatment of symptomatic neurogenic orthostatic hypotension (NOH) in patients with primary autonomic failure (Parkinson’s Disease (PD), Multiple System Atrophy (MSA) and Pure Autonomic Failure (PAF)), Dopamine Beta hydroxylase (DBH) Deficiency and Non-Diabetic Autonomic Neuropathy (NDAN).” In Japan, the drug has been approved for the same indication since 1989, but is marketed at lower doses (100 – 300 mg tid) compared to 100 – 600 mg tid in this development program. In Japan, droxidopa is also approved for “freezing” associated with Parkinson’s disease and for hemodialysis patients, indications that are not being sought in this development program.
L2068: Droxidopa, a prodrug, is converted mostly peripherally, but also centrally (as it passes through the blood brain barrier) into norepinephrine (NE). It is thought that patients with neurogenic orthostatic hypotension lack the ability to autoregulate their blood pressure via appropriate vasoconstriction when they rise from a supine to a standing position. Therefore, it has been thought that vasoconstrictors should convey therapeutic benefit to patients with NOH. Midodrine, the only approved drug for symptomatic neurogenic orthostatic hypotension, is a vasoconstrictor. It was given accelerated approval based on a surrogate endpoint, standing systolic blood pressure, which was thought at the time to be reasonably predictive of a therapeutic benefit. Many years have passed since initial approval (1996) and no studies have convincingly shown that there is symptom relief associated with midodrine use. Other vasoconstrictors, such as ephedrine, have not been able to show symptomatic benefit in patients with NOH. In addition to traditional methods of treatment such as getting up slowly, raising the head of the bed at night, keeping volume expanded by increasing salt and fluid intake, and fludrocortisone as a volume expander (off-label use), there are some other medications that are used off-label by patients who suffer from these conditions. Most of these drugs carry serious safety concerns. At this time, patients with symptomatic neurogenic orthostatic hypotension have few therapeutic alternatives.
L2069: Salient factors in the regulatory history of droxidopa are that 1) FDA agreed upon orphan drug designation for droxidopa and 2) there is an SPA for Study 301 whereupon it was agreed that a highly significant outcome (P &lt; 0.00125) in this one trial might be sufficient for approval. Other correspondence reflected FDA’s interest in assessing the durability of droxidopa’s effect as well as the methods the sponsors used for validating the Patient Reported Outcomes measures that were used as efficacy endpoints. At the pre-NDA meeting, FDA clearly communicated the need to understand the rationale for the sponsor’s claim of clinical benefit associated with the small effect size (0.9 points)
L2070: demonstrated in Study 301 on the primary endpoint [composite Orthostatic Hypotension Questionnaire (OHQ)].
L2071: From a historical perspective, it is important to note that Study 302, a study that enrolled 101 patients in the double-blind phase, was the first pivotal study to be completed. It had a randomized withdrawal design and failed on its primary efficacy endpoint, the Orthostatic Hypotension Symptom Assessment (OHSA) Item 1. The difference between the droxidopa arms and the placebo arm was 0. 6 on the OHSA Item 1, favoring droxidopa, but this difference was not statistically significant. Not only did it fail on this primary endpoint, but it failed to demonstrate an effect on standing systolic blood pressure. An exploratory analysis showed a nominally statistically significant improvement in OHQ. As a result of this finding, the sponsor proposed to change the primary efficacy endpoint of Study 301 which was practically finished at the time, to OHQ. FDA agreed.
L2072: Study 301 was initiated and enrolled 162 subjects in its double-blind phase. Responders to the 1-2 week open-label droxidopa titration phase underwent a wash-out week, followed by double-blind randomization to placebo or the previous titrated dose of droxidopa. The results on both OHSA Item 1 and OHQ were highly statistically significant [p &lt; 0.001 for OHSA Item 1 (effect size 1.3) and p = 0.003 for OHQ (effect size 0.9)]. Also, a placebo-subtracted increase in standing systolic blood pressure (SBP) of 7.3 mmHg was demonstrated, (p &lt; 0.001).
L2073: A third 102 patient study (Study 303) , similar to Study 302, had a randomized withdrawal design and was an extension study of 3 1/2 months duration, enrolling mostly patients from Study 302. The patients were on droxidopa for three months during this study at their titrated dose. After three months they were randomized to continue on their titrated dose of droxidopa or to the placebo treatment arm. This study was not powered to show an effect on OHQ and it did not show an effect on either OHQ or OHSA Item 1. There was a numerical difference between the droxidopa arms and the placebo arm of 0. 4 on the OHSA Item 1, favoring droxidopa. This difference in OHSA Item 1 score was about half what was seen in Study 301. There was also no effect on standing SBP. In fact, there was a seemingly paradoxical effect on systolic blood pressure. The placebo arm showed no change in standing SBP whereas the droxidopa arm actually demonstrated a decrease in standing SBP of 8.4 mmHg (p = 0.29).
L2074: The safety data base of this development program was not robust. The total patient exposure in the Chelsea program was 752 patients with 276 patients exposed \geq 6 weeks and only 64 of those were exposed to the maximum dose of 600 mg tid. A total of only 93 patients were exposed over 1 year and only 26 of those were exposed at the maximum dose of 600 mg tid. There was limited phase 3 double-blind exposure; only 131 patients received droxidopa with a mean exposure of 11 days during the double-blind phase 3 studies. This low extent of long-term exposure makes it difficult to properly evaluate the long-term safety of droxidopa.
L2075: Melanie J. Blank, MDNDA 203202Droxidopa (Northera)
L2076: In addition, the limited exposure to the highest dose, 600 mg tid, makes it difficult to make a proper safety assessment.
L2077: Because this is an orphan indication where exposure is not expected to meet ICH E1 recommendations (300 patients exposed for &gt; 6 months, 100 for &gt; 1 year), this wouldn't have been so problematic except that the safety of droxidopa was not so clean. During the longer term open-label experience with droxidopa, there were several deaths, SAEs, discontinuations for AEs, and events of hypertensive crisis, strokes, and myocardial infarction. There were also several patients with worsening of their movement disorders. Of utmost concern are reports of neuroleptic malignant syndrome from Japan that aren't clearly explained. During a 10-year reporting period, there were 9 cases of neuroleptic malignant syndrome reported while patients were taking droxidopa.
L2078: Hypertension was a safety issue that had to be monitored carefully during the titration phase as some patients had hypertensive responses and required down titration or discontinuation of drug. There were few serious AEs and there were no deaths during the short pivotal trials, Study 301 and Study 302.
L2079: Of note, these events are difficult to interpret because they were largely reported in uncontrolled extension studies, or spontaneously reported from the post-marketing period in Japan.
L2080: The decision whether or not to approve droxidopa is debatable. Arguments in favor are the following:
L2081: 1. There is strong evidence from one randomized controlled clinical trial (Study 301) that droxidopa confers at least one week of symptomatic benefit according to a questionnaire that pertains to the core symptoms of the disorder of symptomatic NOH, the Orthostatic Hypotension Symptom Assessment (OHSA) Item 1 with a robust p value of &lt; 0.001. The mean effect size on this instrument was 1.3 points on a scale of 0-10 where the average baseline reading was between 5 and 6.
L2082: While the clinical benefit associated with the effect size of 1.3 may not be fully understood at this time, this is an average response. Some patients experienced a much greater benefit. An argument can be made that droxidopa is a titratable drug and patients will be
L2083: able to tell whether it is helping them and whether it is worth assuming certain safety risks.
L2084: In the FDA guidance titled, “Providing Clinical Evidence of Effectiveness for Human Drug and Biological Products”, certain factors are suggested to be considered when deciding if one successful trial is sufficient:
L2085: • Large multicenter study where no single study site provided an unusually large fraction of the patients and no single investigator or site was disproportionately responsible for the favorable effect seen ((Given the limited numbers of patients with this condition, this study would have to be considered “large,” and the rest was true))• Consistency across study subjects (This was true)• Multiple studies in a single study (Not the case here)• Multiple endpoints involving different events (Yes, if you include the change in systolic blood pressure)• Statistically very persuasive finding (Yes with p &lt; 0.001 on the OHSA Item 1)2. There is strong evidence from this same study that droxidopa raises standing systolic blood pressure for at least one week. There is also a relationship between systolic blood pressure increase and OHSA Item 1 improvement, albeit a weak one (exploratory analysis by Dr. Zhang shown in Figure 20).3. There is strong evidence from one randomized controlled clinical trial (Study 301) that droxidopa confers at least one week of symptomatic benefit according to a comprehensive questionnaire [Orthostatic Hypotension Questionnaire (OHQ)] that is designed to assess symptoms that are common in patients with symptomatic orthostatic hypotension as well as the impact of those symptoms on ability to function (p value of &lt; 0.003). The mean effect size on this instrument was 0.9 points on a scale of 0-10 where the average baseline reading was between 5 and 6.4. Despite failing to show benefit on its primary efficacy endpoint, there was supportive evidence from Study 302 for benefit on the OHQ with a nominally significant benefit for this endpoint (effect size was 0.96, p = 0.042).
L2086: 5. Most of the individual scores on the questions in OHQ in Study 301 showed a statistically significant benefit.
L2087: Arguments against approval follow:
L2088: 1. There has been no durable effect (i.e., more than 4 weeks) demonstrated for droxidopa and the safety profile is not clear because of the design of the trials. Studies 302 and 303, while showing the slightest of favorable trends on OHSA Item 1 (0.6 effect size, p=0.51 for Study 302 and 0.4 effect size, p = 0.25 for Study 303), did not demonstrate clinical/ symptomatic benefit for droxidopa after two weeks and 3 months, respectively, of chronic use followed by a 2-week randomized withdrawal period. These studies also failed to show any durable effect on systolic blood pressure. The sponsors suggest that there might be a carry-over effect of droxidopa that might obscure benefit in a 2 week randomized withdrawal experience. While this is possible, an alternative explanation could be loss of effect after several weeks of treatment. It is also possible that other study-related effects such as optimization of other aspects of their treatment regimen, including optimization of other medications, increased salt and water intake, increased exercise and elevated head at bed at night may have obscured any additional benefit that droxidopa might have conferred.
L2089: It is important to consider failure to demonstrate durability of effect in light of generally insufficient safety data and concerning safety findings including 18 deaths in the open-label phase, 2 strokes on post-mortem examination and 1 other stroke in a patient who survived, 3 AEs of hypertensive crisis, 1 myocardial infarction (resulted in death), 1 case of coronary artery disease that resulted in discontinuation, 33 cases of worsening of underlying movement disorder including 2 SAEs, in addition to many other SAEs and discontinuations. Additionally, in the Japanese postmarketing experience, there were 9 reported cases of neuroleptic malignant syndrome, an often fatal condition. A few of these cases appeared to have no likely etiology for what is considered to be a serious iatrogenic condition. The data that the sponsor provided was insufficient to conclude or exclude causal relationships. It is difficult
L2090: and imprudent to assign causality to droxidopa because of the mostly open-label design of the study and the nature of postmarketing reporting periods. Nevertheless, the specter of serious safety issues related to droxidopa has been raised and should not be ignored.
L2091: The primary endpoint that was selected for Study 301, the OHQ, was reviewed by the Study Endpoints and Labeling Development (SEALD team reviewer, Dr. Elektra Papadopoulos, and found to be lacking in content validity. The OHDAS questions should have been crafted to measure the functional impact on the symptoms of orthostatic hypotension. It should have included questions that specifically addressed symptoms associated with postural changes and ability to make those postural changes during their daily activities. Instead, it just queried the patients regarding the ability to stand and/or walk.
L2092: The OHSA questions should have been crafted to measure the symptoms that were derived from the qualitative research as most important and bothersome to the patients interviewed. The OHSA questions do not assess some symptoms that were discovered in the qualitative research included in the review (imbalance and falling, for instance). Conversely, some of the OHSA questions assessed some extraneous symptoms that were not discovered in the qualitative research to be most troubling to the patients (difficulty concentrating and head/ neck discomfort). Furthermore, the OHSA question regarding “fatigue” was ambiguous as fatigue can mean weakness or tiredness. Most patients were discovered in the qualitative research to suffer more from tiredness than fatigue.
L2093: The OHSA Item 1, however, captures the most important symptoms of the patients who suffer from symptomatic orthostatic hypotension: dizziness, lightheadedness, feeling faint, or “feeling like you might black out.” The concept of OHSA Item 1 is comprehensive and unambiguous. The symptom assessed by this item is a core symptom of symptomatic neurogenic orthostatic hypotension as assessed by the qualitative research and therefore has content validity.
L2094: Since Study 301 was successful on OHSA Item 1, this issue would not preclude approval. I included this discussion here to make it clear that in my opinion, the droxidopa development was Study 302 is not quite as supportive for approval as it would have been if OHQ could be considered to be a valid study endpoint.
L2095: 2. The effect size of 1.3 in the OHSA Item 1 is not easy to understand. The sponsor did not provide sufficient data to allow one to equate this change in symptom score to clinical benefit. The sponsor was asked to provide data from the droxidopa development program to elucidate this issue. Without understanding the magnitude of benefit, it is hard to weigh safety concerns against efficacy for the purposes of approval and labeling. This is particularly concerning in this setting where the safety database is relatively small and difficult to analyze.3. In the FDA guidance titled, “Providing Clinical Evidence of Effectiveness for Human Drug and Biological Products”, the following is also written: “When considering whether to rely on a single multicenter trial, it is critical that the possibility of an incorrect outcome be considered and that all the available data be examined for their potential to either support or undercut reliance on a single multicenter trial.” Certainly, approval of droxidopa with no postmarketing commitment would leave many safety and durability of efficacy concerns unanswered.
L2096: In summary, in support of approval, the strength of evidence of short-term efficacy for droxidopa is adequate and the safety demonstrated in the short double-blind trials was relatively good. Against approval, durability of effect of droxidopa has not been demonstrated. Additionally, the safety data base was small considering the worrisome safety signals that arose during the open-label phases of the trials which included deaths, strokes, myocardial infarction, progression of underlying disease and hypertensive crisis. Also very worrisome are the Japanese postmarketing cases of neuroleptic malignant syndrome. Some of those cases do not appear to be explicable on the basis of other drugs known to cause the syndrome.
L2097: On the basis of the safety concerns compounded by absence of evidence of durability of effect, my regulatory recommendation is that we should not grant approval for droxidopa at this time.
L2098: 2 Introduction and Regulatory Background
L2099: 2.1 Product Information
L2100: Droxidopa is a synthetic amino acid analog that is directly converted or metabolized to NE in a single step by DOPA-decarboxylase. The conversion of droxidopa to NE can occur peripherally and/or centrally. In addition to its function as an NE precursor, droxidopa promoted the release of NE from the nerve endings in experiments using brain synaptosomes and slices.1
L2101: 2.2 Tables of Currently Available Treatments for Proposed Indications
L2102: The proposed indication for droxidopa is for symptomatic neurogenic orthostatic hypotension (NOH) in patients with primary autonomic failure (Parkinson's Disease (PD), Multiple System Atrophy (MSA) and Pure Autonomic Failure (PAF)), Dopamine Beta Hydroxylase (D\betaH) Deficiency and Non-Diabetic Autonomic Neuropathy (NDAN). There is only one approved drug (midodrine) for this condition that may soon be removed from the market because it has never been demonstrated to have clinical effectiveness (improve symptoms). Midodrine received subpart H accelerated approval in 1996 for its effects on raising systolic blood pressure (SBP) which was considered at the time to be a surrogate marker of effectiveness for symptomatic neurogenic orthostatic hypotension because it was considered likely to correspond to a clinical benefit. The conditions for which the sponsor, Chelsea Therapeutics, Inc., is proposing to have droxidopa indicated are considered to be rare and often disabling and are accompanied by symptoms of dizziness, weakness, syncope and falls. Patients often become confined to a wheel chair or bedridden and suffer from many comorbid conditions such as infection, and fracture. There are many people that develop secondary autonomic failure from other conditions such as diabetic neuropathy, or simply from advanced age. This other group of patients increases the possibility of a wide off-label market for droxidopa.

L2109: 2.3 Availability of Proposed Active Ingredient in the United States
L2110: The active ingredient will need to be manufactured. It is currently not available in the United States except for experimental purposes.

L2112: 2.4 Important Safety Issues With Consideration to Related Drugs
L2113: Midodrine is the only approved drug for symptomatic neurogenic orthostatic hypotension (NOH). It works similarly to droxidopa presumably by causing vasoconstriction. Supine hypertension is a concerning safety issue with midodrine and there is a boxed warning about the increased risk for supine hypertension in the midodrine label. Supine hypertension can theoretically increase the risk of acute and chronic cerebrovascular disease.
L2114: Fludrocortisone is used routinely in patients with symptomatic NOH. Because of the salt and water retention that it causes, it has limited utility in the elderly.
L2115: 2.5 Summary of Presubmission Regulatory Activity Related to Submission
L2116: In the presubmission communications, FDA agreed upon orphan drug designation in a January 17, 2007 correspondence. On May 1, 2007, in the Pre-IND meeting, FDA stated that one study with a p value of ~ 0.00125 might be adequate for approval. On February 15, 2008, FDA agreed upon a SPA for Study 301 and agreed that the length of patient exposure was adequate for the safety evaluation. In a correspondence of January 20, 2010, in response to a major amendment of Study 301, FDA agreed upon a change in primary endpoint (from OHSA Item 1 to OHQ). In the Pre-NDA meeting on December 10, 2010, the FDA reminded Chelsea that one trial is not usually sufficient for approval. FDA asked for validation data for the PRO instruments used in the studies and to provide justification for how the effect size of 0.9 on the OHQ is a clinically meaningful one.
L2117: 2.6 Other Relevant Background Information
L2118: 2.6.1 Description of Symptomatic Neurogenic Orthostatic Hypotension
L2119: Definition of NOH:
L2120: “Orthostatic hypotension (OH) is a reduction of systolic blood pressure (SBP) of at least 20 mmHg or diastolic blood pressure (DBP) of at least 10 mmHg within three minutes of standing. It is a physical sign and not a disease. An acceptable alternative to standing is the demonstration of a similar drop in blood pressure (BP) within three minutes, using a tilt table in the head-up position, at an angle of at least 60 degrees.” (Consensus Statement, 1996)1.
L2121: Orthostatic hypotension may be a severely disabling condition which can seriously interfere with the quality of life of afflicted subjects. Some patients become confined to a wheelchair and some become bedridden. There are no currently available therapeutic options that have been demonstrated to have symptomatic benefit. The limitations of currently available therapeutic options, and the incapacitating nature and often
L2122: progressive downhill course of disease, point to the need for an improved therapeutic alternative.
L2123: Symptomatic neurogenic orthostatic hypotension (NOH) results from failure of the autonomic nervous system to respond appropriately to changes in posture, resulting in orthostatic hypotension (OH) on standing, and often, hypertension when supine. When individuals with NOH move from supine to standing, blood pools in the lower extremities, leading to a drop in BP and symptoms of inadequate perfusion of the brain (dizziness, faintness, lightheadedness, blurred vision, weakness, fatigue), muscles (paracervical or lower back pain), heart (angina), and kidneys (oliguria, azotemia).2 Symptomatic NOH can be a debilitating condition for some patients, in that every time they stand, they experience a sudden, extreme drop in BP that can result in dizziness, impaired vision, weakness, fatigue, an inability to think clearly, as well as a decreased ability to conduct activities of daily living that involve standing or walking. Patients may also lose consciousness and fall, increasing the risk of fractures and head trauma3, factors that contribute to morbidity, disability, or death.4 Fear of these types of injuries can result in patients limiting their activities, which leads to a host of complications ranging from a reduction in muscle mass and overall fitness, to depression, feelings of social isolation, and loss of independence5,6. Furthermore, longitudinal studies have shown that chronic OH increases the risk of mortality7,8,9 stroke10 and myocardial ischemia / infarction.11
L2124: Patients have high intra-individual variability in postural BP12,13 and lose their usual diurnal variability for blood pressure or have reversal with higher blood pressures occurring at night.
L2125: Autonomic dysfunction disorders, encompassing Pure Autonomic Failure (PAF, also called idiopathic OH or Bradbury-Eggleston syndrome), Multiple System Atrophy (MSA, formerly also referred to as Shy-Drager syndrome), Parkinson's Disease (PD), Non-Diabetic Autonomic Neuropathy (NDAN), or Dopamine Beta Hydroxylase (DBH) Deficiency (an enzyme that converts dopamine to norepinephrine [NE]), differ in etiology and pathophysiology; however, each condition is accompanied by a deficiency of NE.
L2126: The diseases classified under primary autonomic failure (PAF, MSA, and PD) are all neurodegenerative and of unknown etiology.
L2127: Non-diabetic autonomic neuropathy can be caused by a number of factors, including autoimmune, environmental, and infectious agents. These conditions are associated with either degradation of peripheral NE nerve function or failure of the central mechanism controlling the release of NE. The cause of DBH Deficiency is a rare genetic mutation that results in the loss of this key enzyme in NE production, resulting in a global NE deficiency and a surplus of the NE precursor dopamine.
L2128: 2.62 Droxidopa Approval in Japan
L2129: Droxidopa was approved in Japan in 1989 for the treatment of orthostatic hypotension in familial amyloid polyneuropathy and Shy-Drager Syndrome (SDS, now known as MSA, multiple system atrophy) and for the relief of frozen gait or dizziness upon standing up associated with Parkinson's disease. It was also approved in Japan in 2000 for the alleviation of vertigo, staggering, dizziness on standing up, lassitude, and weakness in hemodialytic patients with orthostatic hypotension. This product has not been marketed in the U.S. It should be noted that the clinical experience in Japan supports individualized treatment with droxidopa. It is clearly stated in the Japanese label that under no circumstances should the dose exceed 900 mg in 2 or 3 divided doses.

L2148: 4.3 Preclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L2149: Several potential safety issues have been raised during the course of the nonclinical Pharm/Tox review process and are thoroughly discussed in the Pharmacology/Toxicology review by Dr. D. Jensen). These are summarized below:Several potential safety issues have been raised during the course of the nonclinical Pharm/Tox review process and are thoroughly discussed in the Pharmacology/Toxicology review by Dr. D. Jensen). These are summarized below:
L2150: 1. Several potentially genotoxic droxidopa impurities have been identified by structural alerts and computational toxicology assessment. The sponsor has been notified of the need to reduce them to appropriate levels or provide evidence of their safety.
L2151: Additional concerns were posed regarding the potential neurotoxicity of a droxidopa degradant 3,4-dihydroxyphenylacetaldehyde (DOPAL). DOPAL has been reported to possess neurotoxic effects both in vitro and in vivo. Also, it has been shown to be detected in human plasma after oral administration of droxidopa, and has been shown to be markedly elevated in some Parkinson's disease patients treated with droxidopa14.. Since droxidopa crosses the blood-brain-barrier and is likely to be converted into DOPAL in the CNS, the potential for CNS toxicity was raised. The sponsor was asked to provide additional information to help address this issue.
L2152: 2. Data regarding serum metabolites or their levels after repeat dosing in animals was incomplete. Additional information has been requested from the sponsor. Four human metabolites of droxidopa were evaluated by the CDER Computational Toxicology Group. Each of the four metabolites was predicted to be positive in more than one genotoxicity assay, two were predicted to be positive in 2-year rodent carcinogenicity assays, and a third has been previously shown to produce tumors in rats. It should be noted that the two rodent carcinogenicity studies were judged to be negative. One human metabolite was predicted to be teratogenic in rabbits. While the actual rabbit teratogenicity studies were negative, it is not known whether rabbits produce that metabolite. In order to be reassured that this droxidopa metabolite is not teratogenic, it is important to know if sufficient levels are produced in the rabbit to explore this potential safety issue. An assessment of existing metabolite data from animals and humans will be important for evaluating the adequacy of the animal reproductive toxicity studies and of the rodent carcinogenicity assays for human risk assessment. However, the sponsor contends that there is a consistent metabolic pattern for droxidopa across 5 species (mice, rats, dogs, monkeys, and humans) based on available data, and that the negative carcinogenicity and teratogenicity results in animals provide an adequate assessment of human risk.3. Studies in rats showed that interference with L-aromatic-amino-acid decarboxylase with carbidopa diminished or abolished the pressor effect produced by droxidopa. Given that carbidopa is routinely given to Parkinson's disease patients treated with L-DOPA, inhibition of peripheral L-aromatic-amino-acid decarboxylase might limit the efficacy of droxidopa in this patient population.4. Preclinical pharmacokinetic data using lower doses of droxidopa were very limited and there were no toxicokinetic data from higher drug doses that would allow direct comparison of drug exposures during toxicology studies to clinical drug exposures. Until such data becomes available, it will be difficult to relate exposures seen in animals to human therapeutic exposures, although other dose extrapolations (e.g., surface area) may be used.
L2153: 5. Toxicity studies in animals showed a dichotomy between species with regard to the toxicity of droxidopa. Droxidopa was essentially nontoxic at the highest doses tested in 52-week dog studies and in 13-week studies in rhesus monkeys. The highest doses tested in dogs and monkeys were 30-fold greater than the highest recommended clinical dose, when based on body surface area.
L2154: In contrast, all studies in mice and rats, including single-dose studies in each species, demonstrated renal tubular toxicity and cardiac myocyte toxicity. Also, gross renal lesions were also observed in the F1 offspring of female rats dosed with droxidopa on days 7 through 17 of gestation. Renal and cardiac lesions were observed at doses that, based on body surface area, were similar to or lower than the highest recommended clinical dose of droxidopa.
L2155: The reasons for the marked differences in toxicity between the various species were not clear. Cardiac and renal lesions have been shown to be normal age-related degenerative disease processes in rodents, and the drug may simply exacerbate this process. It has been shown that some drugs that exacerbate spontaneous renal disease (i.e., chronic progressive nephropathy) in rats do not affect humans. Also, it has been reported that rats have a much higher density of \alpha 1-adrenergic receptors in the cardiac ventricle than do several other species and that this caused rats to have a greater cardiac inotropic (contractile force) response following \alpha 1-adrenergic stimulation than was observed in the other animal species. This could explain the myocardial damage produced by a drug (i.e., droxidopa) that is a norepinephrine precursor. However, the occurrence of renal and cardiac lesions in young rodent animals during single-dose studies may argue against this premise.
L2156: Given the current uncertainty of these cardiac and renal findings in animals and their relevance for humans, it was recommended by the Nonclinical Reviewer, Dr. Jensen, that the ongoing clinical study in renal-impaired patients be completed before final approval is considered. Also, due to the myocardial necrosis and scarring observed in both rats and mice, it was recommended that the sponsor provide clinical cardiac troponin measurements, as requested previously, or even conduct noninvasive cardiac echocardiography (ECHO).
L2157: (Summarized by T. Papoian from draft Pharm/Tox review by D. Jensen - 1/24/12)
L2158: 4.4 Clinical Pharmacology
L2159: 4.4.1 Mechanism of Action
L2160: • Droxidopa is an orally administered, synthetic amino acid catecholamine acid pro-drug that is converted through the catecholaminergic metabolism system, specifically by L-aromatic-amino-acid decarboxylase (DOPA decarboxylase), to produce norepinephrine (NE). While the mechanism is not exactly known, norepinephrine presumably binds to alpha adrenergic receptors in the vascular smooth muscle of arterioles causing vasoconstriction and consequent elevation of systolic blood pressure. Norepinephrine may also have an effect on venous vascular resistance. By elevating the blood pressure, it promotes the maintenance of cerebral blood flow, thereby lessening the symptoms of neurogenic orthostatic hypotension; primarily dizziness, lightheadedness and syncope.
L2161: Droxidopa crosses the blood brain barrier and therefore may exert its effect both peripherally and centrally by increasing NE production.
L2162: 4.4.2 Pharmacodynamics
L2163: • Droxidopa's average elimination half-life is 2.5 hours. The proposed dosing regimen requires droxidopa to be administered every 4 hours during the day.• The plasma protein binding for droxidopa is concentration dependent (decreases from 75% to 25% with increase in concentrations from 0.1 to 10 ug/ml).• Droxidopa crosses the blood brain barrier in animals and humans.• The major active metabolite of droxidopa is norepinephrine. Other metabolites identified in humans and animals include methylated droxidopa (3-OM-DOPS), vanillic acid (VA) and protocatechuic acid (PA). These metabolites are reported to have some vasomotor activity.• Approximately 70% of droxidopa and its metabolites are excreted in urine in animal studies.• Droxidopa is metabolized by non-CYP mediated pathways and involves catecholamine systems in its metabolism. In vitro studies indicate that droxidopa has low CYP induction or inhibition potential.• The moderate food effect observed for droxidopa with high fat meal (34% and 20% reduction in C_{max} and AUC) are not clinically significant and the phase III trials were conducted without any food restrictions. Therefore, droxidopa can be administered with or without food.
L2164: 4.4.3 Pharmacokinetics
L2165: The PK of droxidopa was studied in single-dose (studies 20/1859-94, 20/1860-94) and multiple-dose (study 101) designs in healthy subjects. There was a dose related increase in exposure up to 600 mg dose as shown in Figure 2. The 900 mg tid dose overlapped with the 600 mg tid dose (not shown).

There was no significant accumulation of droxidopa on multiple dose administration in a TID regimen.
